However, the development of such systems remains limited by the scarcity of datasets that pair whole-slide images with clinically meaningful pathology reports. Instead, existing pathology datasets focus on patch- or slide-level annotations of a single endpoint (e.g., disease class), which do not fully capture the rich information in full clinical diagnostic workflow reports. Here, we introduce TUM-Uteria, a uterine pathology dataset comprising WSIs paired with diagnostic pathology reports at both the case and slide levels, collected from a tertiary medical center. The dataset contains 216 clinical cases, comprising 455 slide-level WSI-report pairs. The dataset underwent a structured multi-stage validation procedure involving board-certified pathologists to ensure reliable annotations. TUM-Uteria supports research in computational pathology, including whole-slide image analysis, multimodal learning, and automated pathology report generation.</p></details> |  |

## Representation Learning
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Generative Representation Learning for Scalable Latent Tokenization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20533v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce an approach for self-supervised representation learning designed to be used as a tokenizer for generative modeling. Our objective supervises a representation loss by factorizing the pointwise mutual information of the data, which we estimate by taking information differences between conditional predictions from a learned generative model. We justify our algorithm theoretically and empirically, demonstrating that our generative approach to representation learning avoids dimensional collapse and feature suppression in cases where commonly used methods like DINO and LeJEPA fail. Moreover, we show our representation learning technique is scalable; expending more compute leads to predictably better latent tokenization. For the first time, we perform a careful analysis of the combined flop efficiency of tokenizer and generative model and demonstrate that our approach is a compute-optimal improvement over direct diffusion for pixel-space generation, obtaining an FID-50K of 2.18 with JiT-L. Applied to ImageNet and the text-to-image dataset GPIC, we show tokenizer encoder scaling laws for diffusability, where extrapolating our approach achieves representations in 7x fewer FLOPs than DINOv2 on ImageNet and 8x fewer FLOPs than WebSSL on GPIC.</p></details> | 22 pages, 8 figures |
| **[Imaging-anchored Multiomics in Cardiovascular Disease: Integrating Cardiac Imaging, Bulk, Single-cell, and Spatial Transcriptomics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.07871v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cardiovascular disease arises from interactions between inherited risk, molecular programmes, and tissue-scale remodelling that are observed clinically through imaging. Health systems now routinely generate large volumes of cardiac MRI, CT and echocardiography together with bulk, single-cell and spatial transcriptomics, yet these data are still analysed in separate pipelines. This review examines joint representations that link cardiac imaging phenotypes to transcriptomic and spatially resolved molecular states. An imaging-anchored perspective is adopted in which echocardiography, cardiac MRI and CT define a spatial phenotype of the heart, and bulk, single-cell and spatial transcriptomics provide cell-type- and location-specific molecular context. The biological and technical characteristics of these modalities are first summarised, and representation-learning strategies for each are outlined. Multimodal fusion approaches are reviewed, with emphasis on handling missing data, limited sample size, and batch effects. Finally, integrative pipelines for radiogenomics, spatial molecular alignment, and image-based prediction of gene expression are discussed, together with common failure modes, practical considerations, and open challenges. Spatial multiomics of human myocardium and atherosclerotic plaque, single-cell and spatial foundation models, and multimodal medical foundation models are collectively bringing imaging-anchored multiomics closer to large-scale cardiovascular translation.</p></details> | <details><summary>The a...</summary><p>The authors are withdrawing this version of the preprint to incorporate extensive updates, refine the multi-modal fusion pipelines, and expand the evaluation benchmarks for cardiac imaging and spatial transcriptomics integration. A revised and updated version will be resubmitted shortly</p></details> |
| **[Contrastive uncertainty learning for robust multi-structure segmentation in lower-pelvic MR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20251v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Boundary delineation of anatomical structures in prostate MRI is often compromised by regional ambiguity. Automated segmentation methods relying on global overlap loss functions tend to be insensitive to surface deviations, leading to significant errors in ambiguous regions. Most existing uncertainty-aware methods treat uncertainty as a post-hoc quality indicator or prediction filter. We propose Contrastive Uncertainty Learning (CUL), a supervised framework that uses predictive uncertainty to directly guide representation learning. Using Monte Carlo Dropout, CUL identifies voxel-wise uncertainty and structures the embedding geometry accordingly: low-uncertainty, correctly predicted features form stable voxels while high-uncertainty or misclassified features are actively repelled, preventing feature collapse at difficult boundaries. We evaluate CUL on a 589-scan 3D prostate MRI multi-structure segmentation task (8 anatomical structures) using a ViT with UperNet decoder architecture. Average Dice ($83.38\%$) remains comparable to the baseline ($83.35\%$), while surface distance decreases significantly ($p<0.001$) across 6 of 8 anatomical structures. A hard-case analysis reveals a $22{\times}$ asymmetry between significantly improved and degraded cases ($18.8\%$ vs $0.9\%$). Ablation studies show that removing uncertainty-guided sample selection eliminates improvements for the transition zone, while replacing the contrastive objective with a boundary loss additionally removes bladder improvements, demonstrating that both uncertainty-guided sampling and the contrastive objective are essential for the observed gains. These findings demonstrate that the proposed uncertainty-guided representation learning is a useful mechanism for reducing the risk of extreme errors in safety-critical medical segmentation</p></details> | UNSURE MICCAI 2026 |
| **[TabFORGE: A Tabular Foundation Model for Generative Modelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09424v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative modelling is a demanding test of foundation models, because it requires robust, holistic representation learning for a given data modality, rather than optimisation for a supervised prediction target alone. While recent work on tabular foundation models has achieved remarkable progress in predictive modelling, generative tabular foundation models remain underexplored. Existing tabular foundation generators, in particular, have not yet consistently matched strong dataset-specific generators in synthetic data quality. A key reason is their insufficient alignment with the inter-feature dependency structure of tabular data, which can reflect underlying causal relationships. In this paper, we address this gap by introducing a novel tabular foundation model, \textbf{TabFORGE}, built on pretrained \textbf{Tab}ular \textbf{FO}undational \textbf{R}epresentations for \textbf{GE}neration. TabFORGE is designed to utilise transferable representations induced by a pretrained structure-aware feature encoder, which captures information useful for characterising inter-feature dependencies. It further decouples latent modelling from decoding through a two-stage design: we first pretrain a score-based diffusion transformer, and then pretrain a denoising-aligned decoder using the denoised latent embeddings. This design elegantly mitigates the distribution shifts in latent embeddings that typically arise between training and inference. We evaluate TabFORGE comprehensively against 22 benchmark methods on 45 real-world datasets. Our results show that TabFORGE effectively learns and leverages generalisable tabular representations, enabling efficient generation of high-quality synthetic tabular data, particularly with strong structural fidelity. Code is available at https://github.com/SilenceX12138/TabFORGE.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by the Fortieth Annual Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2026)</p></details> |
| **[CoTL: Compact Token Learning for 3D Scene Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18446v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present CoTL, a framework that learns a compact set of tokens that preserves scene geometry and appearance from unposed multi-view images. Our encoder compresses the input images into small per-view sets of latent tokens that aggregate information across views to form a view-consistent scene representation. Each additional view expands the representation with only a small, resolution-independent set of tokens, allowing its capacity to grow with the available observations. Experiments on RealEstate10K and ACID show that the resulting compact, unstructured token set supports competitive novel-view synthesis and can serve as the latent space of a generative model for 3D Gaussian scene generation. Together, these results show that 3D Gaussian reconstruction is an effective objective for compact scene representation learning and the learned token space provides a basis for broader downstream tasks in 3D vision.</p></details> |  |
| **[Tactile-Guided Robot Learning: From Representation Learning to Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20134v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tactile sensing is becoming central to robust, adaptive, and dexterous robotic manipulation. Beyond its traditional role in contact detection and reactive control, touch is increasingly used to learn transferable representations, condition manipulation policies, and support embodied physical reasoning. Yet existing surveys largely emphasize tactile hardware and perception, leaving the rapid shift toward learning-based tactile manipulation insufficiently synthesized. This paper reviews tactile-guided skill learning from a learning-centric perspective, organized by the functional role of touch across the learning and control pipeline. We examine tactile representation and policy learning, tactile foundation models, predictive and adaptive control, and generalization across objects, tasks, sensors, embodiments, and simulation-to-real transfer. Across these directions, we identify common design principles and remaining challenges in learning transferable physical representations, integrating semantic reasoning with contact-level control, and scaling tactile learning across heterogeneous data and hardware. Finally, we outline emerging directions toward tactile foundation and world models that can predict, reason about, and adapt physical interactions. Together, this review characterizes the transition of touch from a task-specific feedback signal toward a core modality for general-purpose embodied intelligence. An accompanying database of the reviewed literature and associated resources is available at https://sites.google.com/view/touch-in-robot-learning-review.</p></details> |  |
| **[HRIL: Learning Multimodal Synergy via Higher-Order Tensor Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.12393v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Self-supervised multimodal representation learning has achieved remarkable success across diverse domains, yet capturing synergistic information remains challenging due to the complexity of cross-modal interactions. Unlike the shared information across individual modalities, synergy arises when task-relevant signals emerge only from the joint configuration of multiple modalities and cannot be recovered from any modality in isolation. This work focuses on how to preserve the information capacity for such synergistic signals in multimodal representations. The key observation is that synergistic information is reflected in higher-order statistical dependence among modalities, which provides a principled target for explicitly modeling joint interactions. Motivated by this insight, we propose Higher-order Representation and Information Learning (HRIL), which constructs an empirical cross-moment tensor over modality embeddings to represent multi-way interactions. HRIL employs Tucker decomposition to obtain a core tensor, complemented by a synergy-aware regularizer that prevents energy concentration and preserves higher-order coupling capacity for synergistic information capture. Experiments on the controlled synergy task and real-world benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements over existing multimodal contrastive methods, with notable gains on tasks dominated by synergistic interactions. Code is released at https://github.com/brightest66/HRIL.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at NeurIPS 2026</p></details> |
| **[Discriminative Score Function: Turning Pretrained Models into Functional Generative Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19743v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Discriminative models are trained with task-specific objectives such as classification, detection, or representation learning, yet their practical utility depends on generalizing beyond the training task to unseen samples from the same domain. These results show that discriminative training leaves a generative functional signal for synthesis and calibration. Accepted at the Fortieth Annual Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (2026)</p></details> |

| **[When Treatments Spill Over: A Neural Framework for Spatiotemporal Causal Effect Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16086v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Real-world public health policies are often enacted by local jurisdictions, but their effects can ripple through connected populations. We introduce an LSTM-based representation-learning encoder that integrates each unit's own and neighborhood histories into compact sequential summaries for downstream modeling and causal estimation. These summaries feed a self-normalized kernel inverse-probability-weighting estimator for continuous spillover exposure. Beyond theoretical guarantees, simulations show improved performance over outcome-regression baselines; in a practically motivated county-level COVID-19 policy study, the framework finds both business-and-economic restrictions and education-and-childcare policies associated with lower subsequent case burden, with larger estimates for business-and-economic restrictions. | **[Platonic Projection Structures: Operator-Induced Observability in Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05175v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We characterize observability in representation learning through Platonic Projection Structures (PPS), an operator-theoretic framework for analyzing representation accessibility under partial observation. Rather than treating observable outputs as direct reflections of latent representations, PPS models observation through a self-adjoint positive semidefinite operator acting on a latent representation space. A system is represented as a triple $(H, Π, O)$, where $H$ is a latent representation space, $Π\succeq 0$ is an observation operator, and $O(v)=\langle v,Πv\rangle$ defines an induced scalar observable. Observability is characterized by the quotient geometry $H/\ker(Π)$, representing equivalence classes of latent states indistinguishable under observation. We show that quantum measurement and representation inference under linear observation models share this operator-theoretic structure while differing in the algebraic properties of their observation operators; the correspondence is structural rather than physical. Representation transfer and knowledge distillation can likewise be interpreted as approximate preservation of observable geometry through $ΦΠ_T \approx Π_S Φ$. PPS also reveals a structural limitation of output-based interpretability: latent components in $\ker(Π)$ are inaccessible from induced observables, imposing intrinsic constraints on attribution and explanation methods. Controlled empirical validations demonstrate kernel-invariant observability, projection-induced attribution gaps, and rank-controlled observable geometry in latent representation spaces. PPS thus provides an explicit characterization of observability through operator-induced quotient geometry and a unified perspective on representation accessibility, interpretability, and projection-mediated inference.</p></details> | <details><summary>29 pa...</summary><p>29 pages, 7 figures. Published in Entropy</p></details> |
| **[AVA-Encoder: Towards Agent-Native Video Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.12313v3)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic video creation systems remain far from cinematic-grade quality: their underlying models struggle to plan how story, characters, visuals, and sound fit together, and high-quality creation trajectories to learn such planning abilities are scarce. Reconstruction residuals are distilled into textual gradients that drive dual-loop self-evolution: an outer loop learns a shared encoding policy across videos, and an inner loop refines each video's Film KG at test time. AVA-Encoder improves reconstruction fidelity by 20.7 percentage points over the strongest baseline; its learned policy outperforms a finely human-tuned policy with over 70 percent fewer system-prompt tokens; and Film KGs support coordinated editing and improve all tested downstream creation systems. | **[Neural Supervised Clustering for Learning Individualized Treatment Rules with Many Treatments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19104v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Learning an individualized treatment rule (ITR) with many treatments requires estimating treatment-specific effects from limited observations per arm. NSCF lowers regret relative to a strong neural reference in nonlinear exact-sharing simulations; perturbations characterize the limits of hard sharing. The resulting tokens are processed by a Region-Time Transformer and pretrained with masked reconstruction of latent features and region-level signals. Furthermore, we assess the impact of the six proposed self-supervised training tasks through ablation studies. Empirical evaluations show that, on CogVideoX-2B, MiMaAlign achieves a relative improvement of up to 21.0% in the Physical Commonsense (PC) score on the VideoPhy benchmark over the baseline, and also delivers substantial gains on the highly challenging VideoPhy2 benchmark, while maintaining general video quality on VBench and being supported by blind user comparisons.</p></details> | 20 pages, 14 figures |

We apply our analysis to seven Forward-Forward variants and related layer-local methods and find that initially misclassified points near the probe's decision boundary generally have higher rescue rates than those farther inside incorrect decision regions. A content-independent cutoff separates the visible prefix from the prediction target. This yields stored-suffix invariance: with fixed model state, randomness, prefix, and cutoff, changing the stored suffix cannot change the forecast context. We evaluate autoregressive prediction both in-distribution and zero-shot under unseen temperature and genetic perturbations. Zero-shot refers to evaluation on perturbation conditions never seen during wild-type finetuning, without any additional training. However, Walrus (CRPS) is the strongest model under the most severe distribution shift of unseen samples of mutated embryos and temperature conditions. Interestingly, predictions capture the shape of the mean surface tissue velocity trajectory and its separation between 17°C and 27°C, including the timing of the peak at Germ Band Extension onset, a biologically meaningful milestone of embryo development. Our results provide preliminary support that representations learned from different dynamical systems can transfer to experimental morphogenesis in developmental regimes, provided they admit a continuum description. | **[VGGA: Geometry-Grounded Autoencoder for Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16204v1)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Autoencoders are the core component of latent video generative models, compressing high-dimensional videos into compact latent spaces for downstream generation. Building on this insight, we propose \textbf{V}ideo \textbf{G}eometry \textbf{G}rounded \textbf{A}utoencoder (\textbf{VGGA}), a geometry-aware video autoencoder that explicitly incorporates geometric constraints into the reconstruction process. Specifically, VGGA grounds the learned representations along three complementary geometric dimensions: \emph{Structural Depth}, \emph{Motion Correspondence}, and \emph{Projective Warping}. These objectives are unified within a Video Geometry Decoder, which employs a shared spatio-temporal upsampling trunk to jointly optimize geometric learning and RGB reconstruction. Through these geometry-grounded objectives, VGGA encourages the latent representations to capture the underlying 3D structure and motion of dynamic scenes. VGGA consistently improves video generation across both geometry-centric and human-motion domains, reducing gFVD by 28.1\% on RealEstate10K and 37.7\% on Taichi-HD. In this paper, we propose Sera, a semantic representation aggregation framework that complements temporal modelling with degradation semantics. Experiments on the mainstream benchmark across multiple prediction horizons and different temporal models show that Sera consistently improves forecasting performance, achieving up to a 37.3% reduction in prediction error over the temporal baseline and enhanced generalizability. Counterfactual analysis examines how forecasts respond to changes in degradation semantics to assess interpretability. The results show that prediction responses are consistent with the meanings of key degradation descriptors across tested horizons. Together, these findings demonstrate that structured degradation semantics and effective aggregation can improve forecasting accuracy and support reliable and interpretable battery health forecasting for advanced battery management.</p></details> |  |
| **[Multi-Marginal Inverse Optimal Transport for Contrastive Learning Via Explicit Anchor-Positive-Negative Coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33741v2)** | 2026-10-12 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Inverse Optimal Transport (OT) based methods for representation learning learn representations such that the global OT coupling between a pair of data marginals in the representation space, concentrates on the positive pairs. This is in contrast to previous methods that primarily focused on pairwise matching. To alleviate this, we develop a novel multi-marginal (MM) inverse OT (IOT) contrastive learning (CL) approach called Neg-MMIOT-CL, which learns representations such that the global multi-marginal OT (MMOT) coupling between a triple of data marginals, with respect to a carefully designed ground-cost between triplets of data points in the representation space, concentrates on the anchor-positive-negative $\textit{triplets}$. For a latent class model, we empirically show that Neg-MMIOT-CL alleviates dimensional collapse. Furthermore, for a specific choice of ground cost for all triplets in representation space, we prove that the optimal representation configuration for Neg-MMIOT-CL exhibits equiangular property for within-class and across-class representations, which translates to Neural-Collapse when the representation dimension is larger than the number of classes minus one -- a result that is $\textit{previously established only}$ for pairwise contrastive learning methods. Finally, we propose Neg-IOT-CL-PushPull, that is a computationally efficient alternative to Neg-MMIOT-CL, alleviating the high cost of computing MMOT plans needed during implementation. Within DM, generative learning and representation learning can be viewed as directionally inverse distributional processes: generative learning maps a tractable reference to data, whereas representation learning maps data to a designed reference law. For the Mallows-distance instantiation studied here, we connect the population objective to class-centre separation and classification error and derive a finite-sample bound on downstream classification error. | **[Employing Discrete Fourier Transform in Representation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.06765v2)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Image representation learning via input reconstruction is a common technique in machine learning for generating representations that can be effectively utilized by arbitrary downstream tasks. The DFT provides meaningful global information at each frequency level, making individual frequency components useful as separate learning targets. When dealing with multidimensional input data, the DFT offers remarkable flexibility by enabling selective transformation across specific dimensions while preserving others in the computation. Moreover, certain types of input exhibit distinct patterns in their frequency distributions, where specific frequency components consistently contain most of the magnitude, allowing us to focus on a subset of frequencies rather than the entire spectrum. These characteristics position the DFT as a viable learning objective for representation learning. We validate our approach on the CIFAR-10 and STL-10 datasets under constrained training regimes. | **[FENAS: A Few-Shot Meta-Ensembling Framework for Efficient Neural Architecture Search with Synergized Zero-Cost Proxies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.15307v1)** | 2026-10-11 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Efficient neural architecture search (NAS) requires accurate performance estimation under limited evaluation budgets. | **[What Really Works for Universal Multimodal Embeddings? To address this, we systematically evaluate representative strategies under a unified benchmarking framework on MMEB. Our systematic analysis yields three key insights: (1) Gradient distortion: Upweighting gradients from high-similarity negatives consistently hurts downstream generalization; (2) Distribution illusion: Wider separation between positive and negative similarity distributions does not necessarily translate to better retrieval performance; and (3) Synergistic training: While false-negative mitigation or hard-batch construction yields limited gains in isolation, integrating hard-negative mining, false-negative mitigation, and hard-batch construction achieves optimal performance, delivering an average improvement of 6.0% on MMEB with Qwen2-VL-2B. | **[SemiCodec: Bridging Speech Tokenization and High-fidelity Reconstruction with Semi-discrete Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14834v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Speech representation learning requires a discrete modeling interface while preserving high waveform fidelity. Direct joint optimization may create a reconstruction shortcut through the continuous pathway, leaving the discrete tokens insufficiently grounded. To address this issue, we propose a Preserve-then-Ground training paradigm that first establishes a high-fidelity synthesis space and then grounds SSL-guided discrete tokens within it. Experiments show that SemiCodec outperforms fully discrete codecs in reconstruction quality and intelligibility while remaining competitive with continuous representations in acoustic fidelity and speaker preservation. Audio samples are available at https://pennyw1-se.github.io/SemiCodec-Demo-Page/</p></details> | <details><summary>5 pag...</summary><p>5 pages, 2 figures, 3 tables. | **[PRISM: Precision Forcing for Generalist Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14704v1)** | 2026-10-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Coupling a pretrained vision-language model (VLM) with a diffusion or flow-matching action expert has become the mainstream vision-language-action (VLA) paradigm for embodied policies, yet they remain unreliable once a task demands precise manipulation, e.g., submillimeter cable insertion. Along the generative path, inference integrates a locally supervised velocity field, so equally penalized errors at different flow times can affect the executed action unequally. Consequently, precision-task performance deteriorates as physical tolerance tightens by orders of magnitude. We call this supervision imbalance across contexts and flow times Flow-Time Starvation. The remedy is to jointly align supervision allocation and representation learning with context-dependent precision demand, enabling the policy to recognize when greater precision is needed. We instantiate this precision-forcing strategy as Precision-aware Reweighting and Internalization for Skilled Manipulation (PRISM), which scores precision demand offline, strengthens flow supervision where errors are consequential and action signals are visible, and internalizes that demand in the VLM condition representation. Weaker results on heterogeneous families are traced to unsupervised threshold calibration rather than reconstruction quality, identifying calibration as the dominant remaining detection bottleneck. Accelerated inference via DPM-Solver++ reduces sampling cost by a factor of 3.5 to 5.7 at the dataset level but couples to thresholding behavior, establishing that the sampler and threshold must be validated jointly. | **[Learning Projection-Aware 360-Degree Image Rectification via Dual-Projection Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.00911v2)** | 2026-10-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Panoramic cameras provide a 360° field of view and are widely used in panoramic vision, immersive visual computing, and robotic perception. Numerical continuation experiments show successive loss of latent dimensions near these spectral marginalities. ## Causal Inference
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[Testing Conditional Distribution Equality Using Generative Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20447v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Conditional distribution equality testing is a core problem with applications in causal inference, model diagnostics, and transfer learning. Existing methods often lose power when the conditioning variable is high-dimensional. We propose a new test that leverages generative models to compare conditional distributions using both observed data and synthetic samples. A sample-splitting and studentization scheme is introduced to control estimation error. Under the null, the test statistic is asymptotically normal, and we establish local power against contiguous alternatives. Our theory under the null and fixed alternative requires only consistency of the learned conditional distribution, allowing for a broad class of generative models. Empirical results demonstrate accurate size control and strong power in high-dimensional settings compared to representative kernel-based conditional and joint two-sample tests.</p></details> | <details><summary>18 pa...</summary><p>18 pages, 1 figure, 2 tables</p></details> |
| **[Propensity score with factor loadings: evaluating the effect of the Paris Agreement on the returns of European stocks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.08764v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Factor models for longitudinal data, where policy adoption is unconfounded with respect to a low-dimensional set of latent factor loadings, have become increasingly popular for causal inference. Most existing approaches, however, rely on a causal finite-sample approach or computationally intensive methods, limiting their applicability and external validity. In this paper, we propose a novel method for panel causal inference within a super-population framework, based on inverse propensity score weighting, where the propensity score is a function of latent factor loadings. The approach relaxes the traditional restrictive assumptions of causal panel methods, while offering advantages in terms of causal interpretability, policy relevance, and computational efficiency. Under standard assumptions, we develop a three-step estimation procedure for the ATT and derive its large-sample properties using M-estimation theory. Simulation results show that the proposed estimator remains reliable across a variety of designs, including settings with misspecified factor structure and weakly dependent errors. We apply the method to assess the causal effect of the Paris Agreement - a policy aimed at fostering the transition to a low-carbon economy - on European stock returns. Our empirical results suggest a statistically significant and negative short-run effect on the stock returns of firms that demonstrate a strong commitment to sustainability.</p></details> |  |

However, conventional Gen Odds compares independently drawn treatment and control outcomes and targets a population-level contrast rather than an individual-level contrast. Methods: We defined the Causal Gen Odds using the joint distribution of potential outcomes and identified it under conditional independence of the potential outcomes given baseline covariates. We developed a model-based estimator using nonproportional cumulative logistic regression and introduced an odds-ratio sensitivity parameter to assess departures from conditional independence. We evaluated the estimators through simulations and illustrated them using data from the ACCORD Study. Results: In simulations, the conditional-independence estimator was approximately unbiased with 95.5% confidence interval coverage under conditional independence, but bias and undercoverage increased with residual dependence. When the sensitivity-analysis estimator incorporated the data-generating dependence parameter, bias remained below 0.01 and coverage ranged from 93.7% to 96.1% across examined settings. In the ACCORD Study, the Causal Gen Odds for right-foot pre-tibial edema was 0.909 (95% CI: 0.880 to 0.940) under conditional independence and remained stable across the examined sensitivity range. Under a model imposing only minimal regularity conditions, the target is Hellinger discontinuous when both inverse problems are ill-posed: arbitrarily small changes in the data-generating law can produce arbitrarily large changes in the target, ruling out informative locally uniform inference. Under regularity conditions, pathwise differentiability is restored if the uniformly bounded solution is unique at $P_0$. It is also restored, regardless of uniqueness, under the stronger requirement that the corresponding solution satisfy a uniformly bounded $β$-source condition for some $β$>0. ## LLM
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[One Task, Many Interfaces: Simple Cross-Interface Consistency Training Improves LLM Agent Robustness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20576v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Post-training binds the competence of an LLM agent to the interface it was trained on. Semantics-preserving perturbations of that interface, such as renaming an action or changing the notation, leave the underlying task unchanged yet make the agent worse at it. Scoring each sample independently means interface randomization only broadens this dependence from a single interface to the training interface distribution, so out-of-distribution perturbations still degrade the agent. We propose Cross-Interface Policy Regularization (CIPR), a self-supervised objective that replaces action names with uninformative identifiers and presents each training sample under two interfaces in which equivalent actions are identified by construction. CIPR penalizes the KL divergence between the action distributions induced under the two interfaces, so the policy behaves consistently across them. We evaluate CIPR in both supervised and reinforcement-learning post-training, where it improves robustness. On agentic function calling, held-out interface perturbations reduce the accuracy of a post-trained agent by nearly 25%, and CIPR recovers 10.5% while leaving accuracy on the unchanged interface intact.</p></details> |  |

We apply this method, along with our Extract-Verify-Proof autoformalization pipeline, to construct the Formalized Library of Mathematical Functions (FLMF), a partial formalization of the NIST Digital Library of Mathematical Functions (DLMF). On three labeled datasets, our method achieves the lowest accepted-error rate among the evaluated methods, measured as the fraction of incorrect statements accepted by the verification method. Expert review of the results also identifies translation and source defects in existing datasets. Code available at https://github.com/annamei1/FLMF-Ch7ErrFunc-Release.git.</p></details> | 9 pages |

| **[Relevance Supervision Can Teach Neural Retrievers to Prefer LLM-Generated Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06163v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent studies show that neural retrievers often exhibit source bias, ranking LLM-generated passages above semantically similar human-written ones, which threatens the reliability of modern information access systems. | **[Co-Evolving Acting and Forecasting for Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20597v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training language agents is bottlenecked by the high cost of long-horizon rollouts, yet standard agentic reinforcement learning (RL) learns only from a terminal scalar reward, leaving the supervision in intermediate steps largely unused. Prior work exploits this supervision through value targets that estimate per-step success probabilities or observation targets that fit environment response. Both are hard to construct well: (1) value targets come from the same single scalar signal for each step in the trajectory, and (2) observation targets, such as web pages and terminal logs, are long and noisy and reach only one step ahead. We propose \name, which adds a third kind of multi-step action targets. On successful on-policy trajectories, \name trains the policy backbone to generate the action sequences that follows each history. Each action is also supervised when forecast from the preceding histories, forming a temporal ensemble that the shared backbone absorbs during training, and this enables acting and forecasting to co-evolve. The policy still acts one step at a time at inference, with no overhead. Across five benchmarks (ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, WebShop, $τ^2$-bench, and AppWorld), \name (1) achieves stronger aggregate performance than GRPO and RL baselines with predictive auxiliary targets across simulated environments, while improving overall performance on both realistic applications; (2) delivers up to $1.9\times$ and $2.0\times$ higher sample efficiency than ECHO and GRPO, respectively, on ScienceWorld; and (3) achieves these gains with a single policy backbone, without additional models, separate pretraining, or inference overhead. | **[Price of Anarchy of First-Price Auctions in Agentic Markets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20587v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>An agentic market is a decentralized serving-time market where AI agents bid to claim each user prompt. An agent's bid is based on its expected value for the prompt, which is the expectation of the reward for its response minus its inference costs. First-price auctions (FPAs), in which the highest bidder pays its bid and receives the prompt, are a natural way to allocate prompts in such a market, since they are simple and transparent to run, and do not require central knowledge of private capabilities and costs. However, FPAs are not truthful: participants may bid less than their true value in the hope of a larger profit, and the prompt may then be allocated to someone other than the bidder who values it the most. This welfare loss is quantified by the $\textit{price-of-anarchy}$ (PoA). In this paper, we argue that agentic markets are likely to possess features that reduce the price-of-anarchy. The first is a $\textit{competitive floor}$, which occurs when multiple bidders usually value the prompt above some baseline share of the optimal welfare (e.g., because the best models are often distilled into cheaper alternatives, which perform similarly). We show that competition among them then limits the welfare loss, which can be driven to zero as the floor rises. The second feature is $\textit{near-independence}$ of values after conditioning on the prompt, which occurs when most of the predictable variation in the expected value is determined by the difficulty of the prompt. As the value distribution approaches conditional independence, we can derive an efficiency guarantee that approaches a factor of $1 - 1/e^2$ of the optimal welfare. Thus, both features give rise to welfare lower bounds that are significantly better than the well-known general case of $1 - 1/e$. We validate these results empirically using auctions with strategic bidders simulated on the EmbedLLM prompt routing dataset.</p></details> | 40 pages |

| **[Do AI Agents Gossip? In the Execution role, the harness restores the path that led to that node, and the model carries out the instruction with the usual task tools until it saves a new node or proposes a solution, which Reflection then approves or sends back to another node. | **[What and Where in Identifying and Quantifying Conventions in Humans and AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20205v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Cooperative success rests in part on implicit communication. Partners converge on conventions: regularities that give an action meaning beyond its direct effect. Yet conventions are usually observed only indirectly: a score drop when partners are swapped shows that a convention existed, but not what it was or where it was used. In the card game Hanabi, the exact probability that a play fails can be computed from the hints received and the cards in view. Players who use conventions succeed more often than this prediction allows; the resulting margin is the convention gap. Off-belief learning provides a hierarchy of agents (levels 1-5) whose first level uses no conventions by construction and whose later levels use progressively more. For humans and these agents on one scale, we ask what the conventions are and where they are used. On what: in two independently collected corpora of human games, humans show a larger gap than every agent level. On the conventions both populations use, however, humans do no better than level 2. The human advantage is concentrated in a situation that no agent level learns to use: hints that point to a card other than the ones they touch. On where: conventions depend on what the player already knows about the card, not on how recently it was hinted, and they are used most in the mid-game; humans use them earlier and later in the game than the agents. In interventions on the agents, blocking a single high-gap situation costs little, because meaning reroutes to other situations, but blocking several together costs far more than the sum of the single costs. Thus, the convention gap turns conventions from an inferred cause of score loss into a measurable, comparable, and causally testable object.</p></details> | <details><summary>32 pa...</summary><p>32 pages, 12 figures, 6 tables</p></details> |

Project page: https://bbbbchan.github.io/DeltaStreamer/</p></details> |  |
| **[LAGO: Language Agents with Reusable Growing Options](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19843v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Long-horizon tasks expose a weakness of monolithic tool-use loops: one agent must decompose, execute, and verify a task within a single trajectory. | **[SecureClaw: Clawing Back Control of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09549v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using large language model (LLM) agents face two distinct security failures: unauthorized external actions and exposure of sensitive plaintext inside the runtime before any final output check can intervene. Sensitive reads pass through a trusted gateway that replaces raw values with reference IDs. In the evaluated deployment, the gateway also provides short planning summaries that deliberately release selected information, a process called declassification. Writes that change external state follow a PREVIEW$\rightarrow$COMMIT protocol in which only a trusted executor may execute the exact request authorized by policy. The runtime can still plan over summaries and reference IDs, but cannot directly resolve the IDs into secrets or perform side effects. Across the AgentDojo, AgentLeak, and Agent Security Bench (ASB) benchmarks, SecureClaw is the only defense we evaluate in a common harness that simultaneously retains usable task utility and achieves 0% attack success rate (ASR) on ASB, 0.64% ASR on AgentDojo, and 3.23% overall leak on AgentLeak's attacked parity lane, which measures final-output and internal-relay leakage. To address this gap, this paper introduces \textbf{RuleTrek}, a multi-domain benchmark for agentic rule reasoning in complex scenarios. Its rule-grounded construction pipeline extracts structured rule graphs, generates and incrementally composes rule-specific sub-scenarios while ensuring consistency and solvability. To systematically evaluate agentic rule reasoning in complex scenarios, RuleTrek assesses three capabilities in interactive workspaces: Rule Identification (RI), Rule Deduction (RD), and End-to-End Rule Reasoning (E2E). Experiments on 11 frontier LLMs show that the highest RI and RD scores are only 62.47\% and 60.43\%, respectively, while E2E scores range from 26.97\% to 55.29\%. These results demonstrate that current LLM agents face substantial challenges in rule reasoning over complex scenarios. We make our code and dataset publicly available at \href{https://github.com/yuan247/RuleTrek}{RuleTrek}.</p></details> |  |
| **[Interlock: From Local Correctness to Task Completion in Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19686v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model agents increasingly undertake long-horizon tasks in complex environments characterized by fragmented evidence, partially completed states, and dependencies across resources and artifacts. Existing agents may satisfy individual requirements while failing to maintain these relationships, producing locally correct but incomplete outcomes. These failures arise because task completion depends on relationships among environmental evidence, consequential decisions, and required effects. To address this completion gap, we propose Interlock, a framework for task synthesis that represents each task as a typed completion graph. Grounding edges connect public evidence to decisions, coupling hyperedges impose joint constraints across components, and realization edges connect decisions to all required downstream effects. Interlock compiles this graph into a resettable workspace, a public request, and an evaluation rubric. A source-coverage review checks whether the original requirements survive generation, while a public-input review checks whether the rubric is supported by the materials available to the solver. An agent-as-judge inspects execution artifacts and runs verification scripts, retaining trajectories judged to satisfy all mandatory requirements. To evaluate training utility, we fine-tune two Qwen3.6 backbones on their own accepted trajectories. Across the two backbones, training improves AutomationBench task pass rates by 4.00-4.50 percentage points, JobBench weighted scores by 3.20-6.64 points, and Harvey LAB criterion pass rates by 3.89-4.16 percentage points. These results demonstrate the training utility of the synthesized experience across external task environments.</p></details> |  |
| **[MCAIR: Monitoring and Correcting Agent Unfaithfulness via Internal Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19679v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM)-based agents have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in completing complex tasks through multi-step reasoning and tool interactions. However, recent studies have revealed that LLM-based agents can exhibit unfaithful behaviors, where their final reports fail to faithfully reflect their actual execution trajectories. Specifically, when agents fail to accomplish tasks through the intended sources or procedures, they may resort to alternative sources or strategies while failing to disclose such deviations, leading users to incorrectly believe that the tasks have been completed according to the original requirements. We refer to this specific form of agent unfaithfulness as Agent Completion Unfaithfulness. Detecting and mitigating agent completion unfaithfulness is critical yet remains largely unexplored. In this work, we propose MCAIR, a runtime monitoring and correction framework that leverages internal representations of LLM-based agents. MCAIR uses multi-step internal representations as inputs to train a risk-aware probe, enabling online detection of potential completion unfaithfulness during execution. Furthermore, MCAIR introduces a risk-guided correction loop that prompts agents to review their execution history and mitigate potential inconsistencies between their execution trajectories and final reports. Additionally, we construct ACUD, a dataset for Agent Completion Unfaithfulness detection that provides step-level execution traces, step-level internal representations, and consistency annotations for training and evaluating runtime detection probes. Experiments demonstrate that MCAIR outperforms LLM-as-a-judge baselines, generalizes across prompts and tasks, and effectively reduces agent completion unfaithfulness.</p></details> | 26 pages |
| **[ABot-ER: Unified Embodied Reasoning through Agentic Data-Model Co-Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18355v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We introduce ABot Embodied Reasoning (ABot-ER), a unified embodied reasoning foundation model that breaks the tension between breadth and depth in embodied reasoning. Embodied intelligence demands the joint mastery of perception, planning, and execution, yet reconciling generality with specialization across this spectrum remains an open challenge. Within a single unified model, ABot-ER commands specialist-level depth across all three competencies, spanning spatial, scene, and temporal reasoning in perception; long-horizon action organization in planning; and point grounding and affordance in execution. The Agentic Data-Model Self-Evolution Engine continuously adapts data strategies to the model's evolving capability state. The Task-Conditioned Adaptive Training Strategy uses task tokens to activate lightweight low-rank residuals over a shared backbone, separating updates from heterogeneous output formats while retaining shared embodied knowledge. Across a systematic evaluation on twelve benchmarks covering three core competencies and five capability dimensions, ABot-ER achieves state-of-the-art overall performance across perception, planning, and execution within a unified parametric framework, demonstrating that breadth and depth can coexist in unified embodied reasoning.</p></details> |  |
| **[Measuring and Reducing Unusable Replies of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19659v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>People delegate multi-step work to LLM agents and read only the reply that the agent hands back. Current evaluation scores task completion and the faithfulness of reports, and it leaves open whether the requester can understand and use the reply. We call this property reply usability and measure it with a reader model that takes the requester's position and tests whether the reply can be interpreted from what the requester has seen, is in the requester's language and answers the request. Its agreement with the majority of five annotators lies within the range of the individual annotators. Across eight models from seven families, 54% of 480 replies fail, and the best model still fails on 30%. Failed replies mostly point to material the requester has not seen or use labels that the agent coined during its work. Stating explicit requirements for the reply reduces the failure rate from 0.52 to 0.43 together with errors and omissions, and a secondary model that rewrites the reply reduces it to 0.36, which human annotators confirm in a blind comparison. Reply usability can thus be scored alongside task success and improved with one appended message or one call to a secondary model.</p></details> | <details><summary>17 pa...</summary><p>17 pages, 4 figures, 12 tables</p></details> |
| **[OmniStepBench: Deployment-Aware Agent Evaluation across Modalities and Task Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06059v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>As large language models evolve from question-answering systems into general-purpose agents, evaluation must move beyond static answer correctness to assess multimodal perception, multi-step execution, tool use, and artifact delivery. However, existing benchmarks are often tied to specific task types, execution environments, or scoring protocols, limiting their comparability, interpretability, and reliability for deployment decisions. We introduce \textbf{OmniStepBench}, a benchmark for deployment-aware agent evaluation across modalities and task complexity. Built on a shared OpenClaw execution environment, OmniStepBench organizes 233 tasks selected and adapted from 22 source benchmarks into a $2\times3$ workload matrix defined by input modality and execution form, and evaluates them under a unified contract-based protocol with evidence-based score auditing. We evaluate 30 commercial API models and 12 locally deployed open-weight models (42 models in total) over 9{,}218 applicable model--task runs, reporting accuracy and token consumption alongside reference costs for API models. Results show workload-dependent rankings, distinct accuracy--cost trade-offs between text and multimodal tasks, and competitive but weaker overall performance from local models. These findings support deployment decisions based on workload profiles, deployment mode, and accuracy--cost trade-offs rather than a single aggregate score.</p></details> |  |
| **[ANCHOR-RE: An Agentic Biomedical Relation Extraction Framework with Retrieval Augmentation and Error-Driven Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03154v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Biomedical relation extraction (BioRE) supports knowledge graph construction and hypothesis generation. In real-world settings, most candidate entity pairs are unrelated. Symbolic systems such as SemRep offer high precision and interpretability but limited recall, whereas large language models (LLMs) can produce false-positive relations. We developed ANCHOR-RE, an agentic framework that combines retrieved demonstrations, ontology-guided semantic constraints, external biomedical knowledge, and error-driven verification to improve BioRE reliability without task-specific fine-tuning. We evaluated ANCHOR-RE on SemRepGS, DDI, and ChemProt using proprietary and open-weight LLMs. To assess generalizability while reducing potential pretraining contamination, we also conducted a post-cutoff temporal precision evaluation using 100 biomedical articles published in 2026. With the proprietary backbone, ANCHOR-RE improved micro-F1 over direct prompting from 0.654 to 0.676 on SemRepGS, from 0.769 to 0.881 on DDI, and from 0.937 to 0.941 on ChemProt, outperforming previously reported inference-only methods on DDI and ChemProt. Gains were also observed with the open-weight backbone. Additional experiments demonstrated cross-family model compatibility and sensitivity to external knowledge quality. Manual assessment of 500 randomly sampled positive predictions from the post-cutoff set yielded 69% precision. These results show that contextual evidence and error-driven verification can improve BioRE reliability across benchmarks, model families, and newly published literature, supporting ANCHOR-RE as a practical training-free approach to biomedical literature mining.</p></details> | <details><summary>Submi...</summary><p>Submitted to Journal of Biomedical Informatics (under review)</p></details> |
| **[ANCHOR: Assessing Naturalistic Constraint Hierarchies and Ordering in Repository-Grounded Agentic Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16859v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>For repository-grounded coding agents, a functionally correct patch is not necessarily an acceptable one: developers also constrain how the task must be carried out-modification scope, workflow order, validation, tool use, and reporting. This capability, which we call engineering constraint following, remains largely invisible to current evaluations. We introduce ANCHOR (Assessing Naturalistic Constraint Hierarchies and Ordering in Repository-Grounded Agentic Coding), a benchmark built on naturalistic, multilingual repository tasks spanning eight language groups. ANCHOR combines (i) a hierarchical taxonomy of 10 categories and 78 leaf types distilled from real developer interactions, (ii) an automated pipeline that instantiates task-grounded atomic constraints and composes them into typed AND/THEN/PRIORITY relations, and (iii) a trajectory-grounded evaluation framework that checks both the code diff and the execution trajectory with rule-based and LLM judges. Across 19 agent configurations spanning 2 scaffolds and 10 foundation models on 358 tasks, the strongest measured system, Claude Opus 4.8 under Cline, reaches 70.1% atomic compliance and 63.7% relation compliance, while 19.0% of tasks pass every scored checklist item under the strict all-item FCCR criterion. A five-engineer audit of constraints from 50 instances reports 94% feasibility, 94% repository consistency, and 96% realism. In the primary paired set (14 configurations), the mean signed change in task completion after adding constraints is +0.7 percentage points; individual changes vary in both directions. These results distinguish functional success from constraint compliance, with failures concentrated in workflow ordering, tool-use discipline, and reporting, and substantial interactions between models and scaffolds.</p></details> |  |
| **[Model What Matters: Decision-Guided Selective World Modeling for Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19642v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reinforcement learning provides a direct mechanism for optimizing task objectives in large language model-driven agents, but task rewards are typically sparse trajectory-level signals that only evaluate task success, without directly supervising action-conditioned environment transitions, leaving the model with limited explicit understanding of action consequences and environment dynamics. Recent work introduces auxiliary supervision through policy and world modeling co-training, yet typically treats complete environmental observations uniformly as world modeling targets, overlooking the varying decision relevance of observation content. We propose DGS-WM (Decision-Guided Selective World Modeling), a decision-guided policy-world modeling co-training approach for language agents. DGS-WM employs a Decision-Guided Observation Selection component that filters observation units with decision value as world modeling targets based on their support for subsequent policy responses, and a Confidence-Adaptive Weighting component that adaptively adjusts supervision strength according to the model's prediction confidence on selected targets. Together, these components form a decision-aware auxiliary world modeling loss, jointly optimized with the RL loss on the same policy model. Across multiple benchmarks covering single-hop/multi-hop question answering and interactive web tasks, DGS-WM achieves consistent improvements under different model scales and RL algorithms, demonstrating more stable and effective performance compared to full-observation world modeling methods.</p></details> | <details><summary>Core ...</summary><p>Core implementation included as ancillary files</p></details> |
| **[SEDM: Selective Self-Evolving Distributed Memory for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.09498v4)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents in a society can reuse each other's experience, but indiscriminate memory use increases processing cost and can reduce task success. We present SEDM (Selective Self-Evolving Distributed Memory), a framework that selects both what agents share and when recipients use it. On the sender side, SEDM admits memories through paired execution tests, ranks them by relevance and measured utility, and abstracts them for cross-domain reuse. On the recipient side, Calibrated Memory Gating (CMG) predicts success with and without memory in a single forward pass, learning from paired outcomes including ties. It injects memory only when the calibrated success gain outweighs a context-cost penalty. SEDM exceeds the strongest evaluated baseline on LoCoMo open-domain questions by 15.1 F1 points and outperforms G-Memory on FEVER and HotpotQA with 16-32% fewer prompt tokens. In a controlled HotpotQA study, CMG achieves success comparable to always using memory with 18% fewer agent input tokens. Within the full SEDM pipeline on LoCoMo, CMG outperforms both always-use and no-memory policies for two SEDM memories while using 19-31% fewer input tokens than always-use. These results show that selective use can recover value from shared memories that hurt performance when injected indiscriminately.</p></details> |  |
| **[J-tau: A Fully Localized Japanese Benchmark for Tool-Agent-User Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19638v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We present J-tau, the first publicly available Japanese benchmark for evaluating tool-using LLM agents in interactive customer-support scenarios. While tau-bench is widely used to evaluate LLM agents, it is English-centric and cannot evaluate agents in other languages. Existing multilingual extensions offer no public Japanese version and rely primarily on machine translation, leaving locale-specific entities such as addresses and currencies inconsistent with the target region. To enable reliable evaluation in Japanese, we carefully localize tau-bench through machine translation, entity adaptation, and human review. During this process, we also uncover and fix two flaws in the original tau-bench: invalid tasks and a score aggregation error that inflates certain models' results. Experiments show that J-tau yields fewer evaluation errors than a naively machine-translated counterpart and that our fixes resolve both flaws.</p></details> |  |
| **[Beyond Perfect Teammates: Multi-Agent Collaboration with Unreliable Colleagues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16117v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based multi-agent collaboration is increasingly used to solve complex tasks by dividing work among specialized agents and integrating complementary expertise. Yet existing systems and evaluations largely rest on an implicit perfect-colleague assumption: collaborators provide correct and complete information and respond promptly to requests. In realistic teamwork, however, collaborators may return information that does not answer the intended question, provide insufficient detail to support a decision, or fail to respond to a request. To separate task-solving competence from the ability to recover an unreliable evidence-gathering process, we introduce the Unreliable Colleagues Benchmark (UnCoBench), which organizes behavioral failures through Misaligned, Incomplete, and Missed settings. The main agent need to verify contextual correctness, diagnose information gap and track unresolved dependencies in order to obtain the correct evidence for solving the task. We also introduce a verifiable and scalable automated generation pipeline that pairs LLM-based scenario generation with executable checks of answer uniqueness and role necessity. Results across ten models show that similar reliable-setting performance can result in different robustness to unreliable evidence. Trace analysis reveals frequent omissions of corrective follow-up, and the recovery behavior involves inefficient waiting and redundant queries. These findings show that robust collaboration requires not only sound reasoning, but also targeted action to diagnose evidence failures, recover relevant information, and integrate it into decisions. Our data and source code are available at https://github.com/iNLP-Lab/UnCoBench.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages, 8 figures, 8 tables</p></details> |
| **[F2A-ObjectNav: An Adaptive Agentic Architecture for Object-Goal Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19618v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Object-goal navigation requires an embodied agent to explore an unfamiliar environment, identify a target object, navigate toward candidate instances, and reliably determine whether the observed object satisfies the mission goal. This work investigates this problem through a two-stage development process. In Stage I, a fixed modular framework is used to examine combinations of exploration, object detection, and target validation methods. The results reveal clear trade-offs among navigation performance, validation reliability, execution time, and model-related cost, with no single fixed configuration remaining preferable across all evaluated conditions. These observations motivate F2A-ObjectNav (Fixed-to-Adaptive Object-Goal Navigation), an adaptive agentic architecture in which a rule-based Supervisor coordinates specialized components for navigation and exploration, object detection, target validation, semantic guidance, and Mission Memory. The architecture uses event-driven recovery, strategy revision, candidate management, and conditional semantic guidance while retaining lightweight execution during normal operation. F2A-ObjectNav is evaluated over 25 episodes in each of AI2-THOR, RoboTHOR, and ProcTHOR, achieving success rate (SR) / success-weighted path length (SPL) values of 0.84/0.52, 0.40/0.22, and 0.44/0.37, respectively. In addition to task-level metrics, architecture-specific behavioral metrics are used to characterize semantic-guidance usage, strategy switching, recovery activation, memory-supported behavior, and decision stability. The results show different patterns of adaptive intervention across the three environments, with higher intervention frequencies observed in RoboTHOR and ProcTHOR than in AI2-THOR.</p></details> |  |
| **[Divergent Paths: Learning an Outcome-Predictive Representation of LLM Agent Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19614v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>In this work, we reframe agentic trajectories as paths through a shared embedding space of actions and environmental observations. We show that successful and failed rollouts of the same task separate in this space, with simple per-task discriminators predicting held-out rollout outcomes at up to 0.95 AUROC across three agent benchmarks and three policy models. To exploit this signal across tasks, we introduce Precog-RM, a transformer-based model that meta-learns a representation in which outcome prediction can be performed in a few-shot setting. We show that Precog-RM generalizes to unseen tasks and models, and gives confident predictions of trajectory success long before termination. Deployed as an early stopping controller for Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) on Tau2-Bench, Precog-RM cuts training FLOPs by up to 47.3\% while maintaining performance comparable to baseline GRPO.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 8 pages appendix</p></details> |
